(3.32)

Therefore, we find a phase transition on the line
\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:x_{1}=0\}
, as it happens in the elliptic case with the rescaling of the stationary wave. On the contrary, for any
t>0
we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{x_{1}\in(-1,1)}\lVert w_{\varepsilon}(t,x_{1},\cdot)-\mathfrak{q}^{-}(\varepsilon^{-1}(\cdot+\tau^{-}))\rVert_{L^{\infty}((-1,1),{\mathbb{R}}^{k})}=0.

(3.33)

That is, for positive time and small
\varepsilon
, the rescaled solutions tend to look like the globally minimizing heteroclinic at the limit
x_{1}\to\pm\infty
, in contrast with what is observed for
t=0
. In terms of the
interfacial density
, the previous means that an initial condition with non-constant density gives a solution with constant density for
t>0
. This phenomenon is probably explained by some kind of parabolic regularization effects. To conclude this paragraph, notice that the considerations presented here are obtained by direct scaling computations. That is, they do not depend on the way
\mathfrak{U}
is obtained. It is only required that
\mathfrak{U}
solves (
3.28
) with conditions (
3.30
). In particular, the assumption
(H6)
is not relevant here and the same would apply to profiles obtained by different means under some other type of assumptions.

4
The abstract setting

4.1
Main definitions and notations

As we advanced in the introduction, instead of proving directly Theorems
1
,
2
and
4
, we will prove a set of more general results which will allow us to deduce the original ones as particular cases. In particular, we introduce an abstract setting similar to the ones considered in
[
57
]
and specially
[
69
]
. The proof of the main abstract results, Theorems
5
,
6
and
7
below, are thus the core of the paper. The passage between the abstract and the original setting is established in section
6
, which in turn proves Theorems
1
,
2
and
4
.

As we said before, the abstract results should be thought as an extension of the work by Alikakos and Katzourakis
[
7
]
to curves taking values in a more general Hilbert space and with minimum
sets
instead of isolated minimum
points
. In fact, we essentially perform an adaptation of their strategy of proof, which turns out to carry on to our setting. That is, our approach will consist on establishing existence of a pair
(c,\mathbf{U})
in
(0,+\infty)\times X
which fulfills

\mathbf{U}^{\prime\prime}-D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U})=-c\mathbf{U}^{\prime}\mbox{ in }{\mathbb{R}}

(4.1)

and satisfies the conditions at infinity

\exists T^{-}\in{\mathbb{R}}:\forall t\leq T^{-},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathbf{U}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2},

(4.2)

\exists T^{+}\in{\mathbb{R}}:\forall t\geq T^{+},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathbf{U}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2}.

(4.3)

Notice that this problem can also be thought as a heteroclinic connection problem on Hilbert spaces for a second order potential system with friction term. Such a problem could have its own interest besides the main application to the existence of traveling waves that we give here. Of course, analogous considerations can be also applied to the results in
[
7
]
as well as our companion paper
[
59
]
.

The nature of the objects introduced above will be made precise along this paragraph. Let
\mathscr{L}
be a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{L}}
and induced norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}
. Let
\mathscr{H}\subset\mathscr{L}
a Hilbert space with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathscr{H}}
. In the original setting,
\mathscr{L}
is
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
and
\mathscr{H}
is
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
, both endowed with their natural inner products. We will take
\mathcal{E}:\mathscr{L}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
an
unbalanced
potential. In the setting of Theorem
1
,
\mathcal{E}
will
essentially
coincide with
E-\mathfrak{m}^{+}
in
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
and with
+\infty
elsewhere
2
2

2

this statement is not exact as the energy
E
is not defined in
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
, but on an affine space based on
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
. However, we can trivially obtain a functional defined on
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
from
E
. See subsection
6.1
.
. Here we just impose a set of abstract assumptions on
\mathcal{E}
. Most of those assumptions follow bi combining ideas in
[
7
]
with ideas in Schatzman
[
67
]
and Smyrnelis
[
69
]
. We will begin by fixing two sets
\mathscr{F}^{-}
and
\mathscr{F}^{+}
in
\mathscr{L}
. For
r>0
, we define

\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r}:=\left\{v\in\mathscr{L}:\inf_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}}\lVert v-\mathbf{v}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq r\right\},

(4.4)

and

\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H},r}:=\left\{v\in\mathscr{H}:\inf_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}}\lVert v-\mathbf{v}\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}\leq r\right\},

(4.5)

that is, the closed balls in
\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{H}
respectively, with radius
r>0
and center
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
. The main assumption reads as follows:

(H1’)
.

The potential
\mathcal{E}
is weakly lower semicontinuous in
\mathscr{L}
. The sets
\mathscr{F}^{-}
and
\mathscr{F}^{+}
are closed in
\mathscr{L}
. There exists a constant
a<0
such that

\forall v\in\mathscr{L},\forall\mathbf{v}^{-}\in\mathscr{F}^{-},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(v)\geq\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{v}^{-})=a

(4.6)

and each
\mathbf{v}^{+}\in\mathscr{F}^{+}
is a local minimizer satisfying
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{v}^{+})=0
. Moreover, there exist two positive constants
r_{0}^{-}
,
r_{0}^{+}
such that
\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{-}}\cap\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{+}}=\emptyset
(see (
4.4
)). There also exist
C^{\pm}>1
such that

\forall v\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}},\hskip 5.69054pt(C^{\pm})^{-1}\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(v,\mathscr{F}^{\pm})^{2}\leq\mathcal{E}(v)-\min\{\pm(-a),0\}.

(4.7)

Moreover, for any
v\in\mathscr{F}_{r_{0}^{\pm}}^{\pm}
, there exists a unique
\mathbf{v}^{\pm}(v)\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
such that

\lVert v-\mathbf{v}^{\pm}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}=\inf_{\mathbf{v}^{\pm}\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}}\lVert v-\mathbf{v}^{\pm}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}.

(4.8)

Moreover, the projection maps

P^{\pm}:v\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}}\to\mathbf{v}^{\pm}(v)\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}

(4.9)

are
C^{2}
with respect to the
\mathscr{L}
-norm.

Hypothesis
(H1’)
defines
\mathcal{E}
as an unbalanced double well potential with respect to
\mathscr{F}^{-}
and
\mathscr{F}^{+}
and gives local information of the minimizing sets. Compare with
(H5)
and the remarks that follow. We have the following immediate consequence, which will be useful in the sequel:

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume that
(H1’)
holds. If we define for
r\in(0,r_{0}^{\pm}]
we define

\kappa^{\pm}_{r}:=\inf\{\mathcal{E}(v):\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(v,\mathscr{F}^{\pm})\in[r,r_{0}^{\pm}]\}

(4.10)

then we have
\kappa^{\pm}_{r}>\min\{\pm(-a),0\}
. Moreover,

\forall v\in\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(v)\geq 0.

(4.11)

Proof.

It follows directly from (
4.7
) in
(H1’)
.
∎

We now impose the following regarding the relationship between
\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{H}
:

(H2’)
.

We have that
\mathscr{H}=\{v\in\mathscr{L}:\mathcal{E}(v)<+\infty\}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}
. In particular,
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}\subset\mathscr{H}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{E}
is a
C^{1}
functional on
(\mathscr{H},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}})
with differential
D\mathcal{E}:v\in\mathscr{H}\to D\mathcal{E}(v)\in\mathscr{H}^{\prime}
, where
\mathscr{H}^{\prime}
is the (topological) dual of
\mathscr{H}
. Furthermore, there exists an even smaller space
\tilde{\mathscr{H}}
with an inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\tilde{\mathscr{H}}}
and associated norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\tilde{\mathscr{H}}}\geq\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}
such that we can find a continuous correspondence

D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}:v\in(\tilde{\mathscr{H}},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\tilde{\mathscr{H}}})\to D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}(v)\in(\mathscr{L},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{L}})

(4.12)

such that

\forall v\in\tilde{\mathscr{H}},\forall w\in\mathscr{H},\hskip 5.69054ptD_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}(v)(w)=D\mathcal{E}(v)(w).

(4.13)

Notice that in the context of Theorem
1
assumption
(H2’)
is easily verified. The space
\tilde{\mathscr{H}}
will be chosen
H^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{k})
and (
4.13
) is no other that integration by parts. The notation
D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}
is chosen to emphasize the formal
\mathscr{L}
-gradient flow structure of the corresponding abstract evolution equation. We now continue by imposing a compactness assumption on
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
:

(H3’)
.

\mathscr{L}
-bounded subsets of
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
are compact with respect to
\mathscr{H}
-convergence.
3
3

3

hence, they are in particular compact with respect to
\mathscr{L}
-convergence

Assumption
(H3’)
readily implies the following:

Lemma 4.2
.

Assume that
(H1’)
and
(H3’)
hold. Then, the sets
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}/2}
defined in (
4.4
) are closed in
\mathscr{L}
.

Assumption
(H3’)
is necessary in order to establish the conditions at infinity. In the main context, it is a straightforward consequence of the compactness of the minimizing sequences. Subsequently, we impose the following:

(H4’)
.

Assume that
(H1’)
holds. For
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
, one of the two following alternatives holds:

1.

\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
is
\mathscr{L}
-bounded.

2.

For all
(v,\mathbf{v}^{\pm})\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}}\times\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
, there exists an associated map
\hat{P}^{\pm}_{(v,\mathbf{v}^{\pm})}:\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{L}
such that

P^{\pm}(\hat{P}^{\pm}_{(v,\mathbf{v}^{\pm})}(v))=\mathbf{v}^{\pm}

(4.14)

and

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\hat{P}^{\pm}_{(v,\mathbf{v}^{\pm})}(v),\mathscr{F}^{\pm})=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(v,\mathscr{F}^{\pm}).

(4.15)

Moreover,
\hat{P}^{\pm}_{(v,\mathbf{v}^{\pm})}:\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{L}
is differentiable and

\forall(w_{1},w_{2})\in\mathscr{L}^{2},\hskip 5.69054pt\lVert D(\hat{P}^{\pm}_{(v,\mathbf{v}^{\pm})})(w_{1},w_{2})\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}=\lVert w_{2}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}

(4.16)

\mathcal{E}(\hat{P}_{(v,\mathbf{v}^{\pm})}(v))=\mathcal{E}(v).

(4.17)

Essentially, in 2. we impose that the projections
P^{\pm}
from
(H1’)
are, in some sense, invertible. Again, this is straigtforward in the concrete setting, as the projections
P^{\pm}
consist on performing a translation. We now impose an assumption for the sets
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H},r_{0}}
:

(H5’)
.

For any
v\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{H},r_{0}^{\pm}}^{\pm}
, as defined in (
4.5
), there exists a unique
\mathbf{v}_{\mathscr{H}}^{\pm}(v)\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
such that

\lVert v-\mathbf{v}_{\mathscr{H}}^{\pm}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}=\inf_{\mathbf{v}^{\pm}\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}}\lVert v-\mathbf{v}^{\pm}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}.

(4.18)

Moreover, the projection maps

P^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H}}:v\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H},r_{0}^{\pm}}\to\mathbf{v}_{\mathscr{H}}^{\pm}(v)\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}

(4.19)

are
C^{1}
with respect to the
\mathscr{H}
-norm. Moreover, if
C^{\pm}>1
is the constant from
(H1’)
, we have

\forall v\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H},r_{0}^{\pm}},\hskip 5.69054pt\lVert P^{\pm}(v)-P^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H}}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}\leq C^{\pm}\lVert v-P^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H}}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}.

(4.20)

Furthermore, for each
r^{\pm}\in(0,r_{0}^{\pm}]
there exist constants
\beta^{\pm}(r^{\pm})>0
such that in case that
v\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}}
satisfies

\mathcal{E}(v)\leq\min\{\pm(-a),0\}+\beta^{\pm}(r^{\pm}),

(4.21)

then
v\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{H},r}^{\pm}
. Finally, we have the following

\forall v\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{H},r_{0}^{\pm}}^{\pm},\hskip 5.69054pt(C^{\pm})^{-2}\lVert v-P^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H}}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}\leq\mathcal{E}(v)-\min\{\pm(-a),0\}\leq(C^{\pm})^{2}\lVert v-P^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H}}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}.

(4.22)

Assumption
(H5’)
is made in order to ensure the suitable local properties around
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
in
\mathscr{H}
. In the main setting, those are known results which follow essentially from the spectral assumption by Schatzman
[
67
]
. Before introducing the last assumptions, we need some additional notation. For
U\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L})
and
c>0
, we (formally) define

\mathbf{E}_{c}(U):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U)(t)dt:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\left[\frac{\lVert U^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{E}(U(t))\right]e^{ct}dt.

(4.23)

More generally, for
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
a non-empty interval and
U\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(I,\mathscr{L})
, put

\mathbf{E}_{c}(U;I):=\int_{I}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U)(t)dt.

(4.24)

Notice that the integrals defined in (
4.23
) and (
4.24
) might not even make sense in general due to the fact that
\mathcal{E}
has a sign. Nevertheless, we can define the notion of
local minimizer
of
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\cdot;I)
as follows:

Definition 4.1
.

Assume that
(H1’)
and
(H2’)
hold. Let
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
be a bounded, non-empty interval. Assume that
U\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(I,\mathscr{L})
is such that
E_{c}(U;I)
is well-defined and finite. Assume also that there exists
C>0
such that for any
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}(\mathrm{int}(I),(\mathscr{H},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}))
such that

\max_{t\in I}\lVert\phi(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}<C,

(4.25)

the quantity
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U+\phi;I)
is well-defined and larger than
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U;I)
. Then, we say that
U
is a
local minimizer
of
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\cdot;I)
.

We assume the following property for local minimizers:

(H6’)
.

Assume that
(H1’)
and
(H2’)
hold. There exists a map
\mathfrak{P}:\mathscr{L}\to\mathscr{L}
such that

\forall v\in\mathscr{L},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(\mathfrak{P}(v))\leq\mathcal{E}(v)\mbox{ and }\mathcal{E}(\mathfrak{P}(v))=\mathcal{E}(v)\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{P}(v)=v,

(4.26)

\forall(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathscr{L}^{2},\hskip 5.69054pt\lVert\mathfrak{P}(v_{1})-\mathfrak{P}(v_{2})\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\lVert v_{1}-v_{2}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}},

(4.27)

and

\mathfrak{P}|_{\mathscr{F}^{\pm}}=\mathrm{Id}|_{\mathscr{F}^{\pm}}.

(4.28)

Let
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, possibly unbounded and non-empty. Let
c>0
. If
\mathbf{W}\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(I,\mathscr{L})
is a local minimizer of
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\cdot;I)
in the sense of Definition
4.1
, which, additionally, is such that for all
t\in I
,
\mathbf{W}(t)=\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{W}(t))
, then
\mathbf{W}\in\mathcal{A}(I)
where for any open set
O\subset{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathcal{A}(O)
is defined as

\mathcal{A}(O):=\mathcal{C}^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(O,\mathscr{L})\cap\mathcal{C}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(O,(\mathscr{H},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}))\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(O,(\tilde{\mathscr{H}},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\tilde{\mathscr{H}}}))

(4.29)

and
\mathbf{W}
solves

\mathbf{W}^{\prime\prime}-D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{W})=-c\mathbf{W}^{\prime}\mbox{ in }I,

(4.30)

where
D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}
was introduced in (
4.12
).

In the context of Theorem
1
,
(H6’)
is a consequence of classical elliptic regularity results as well as properties on the energy functional. The purpose of the projection
\mathfrak{P}
is technical, and in the main setting it will mean that constrained minimizers are bounded with respect to the
L^{\infty}
norm. Before stating the abstract result, we introduce the following constants (assuming that all the previous assumptions hold) which are obviously analogous with those introduced in subsection
2.5
:

\eta_{0}^{-}:=\min\left\{\sqrt{e^{-1}\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{4}\sqrt{2(\kappa^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/4}-a)}},\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{4}\right\}>0,

(4.31)

\hat{r}^{-}:=\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{C^{-}+1}>0

(4.32)

\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}:=\frac{1}{(C^{-})^{2}(C^{-}+1)}\min\left\{\frac{(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}}{4},\kappa_{\eta_{0}^{-}}^{-}-a,\beta^{-}(\hat{r}^{-}),\beta^{-}(\eta_{0}^{-})\right\}>0,

(4.33)

\mathsf{C}^{\pm}:=\frac{1}{2}(C^{\pm})^{2}((C^{\pm})^{2}+(C^{\pm}+1)^{2})>0,

(4.34)

\gamma^{-}:=\frac{1}{C^{-}+\mathsf{C}^{-}}>0

(4.35)

and

d_{0}:=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2},\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2})>0,

(4.36)

where the constants
C^{-},\beta^{-}(\hat{r}^{-}),\beta^{-}(\eta_{0}^{-})
are those from
(H5’)
and
\kappa_{r}^{\pm}
for
r>0
are defined in (
4.10
). The fact that
d_{0}>0
follows from Lemma
4.2
and
(H1’)
. We can finally state the following assumption:

(H7’)
.

Assume that
(H1’)
and
(H2’)
hold. Moreover, assume that

-a<\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}

(4.37)

and

\{v\in\mathscr{H}:\mathcal{E}(v)<0\}\subset\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2}.

(4.38)

Assumption
(H7’)
is essentially the abstract version of
(H6)
.

4.2
Statement of the abstract results

Let us define the space

\displaystyle X:=\left\{U\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L}):\exists T\geq 1,\right.

\displaystyle\forall t\geq T,\hskip 5.69054pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{+})\leq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2},

(4.39)

\displaystyle\left.\forall t\leq-T,\hskip 5.69054pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})\leq\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}\right\}.

(4.40)

The statement of the main abstract result is as follows:

Theorem 5

(Main abstract result)
.

Assume that
(H3’)
,
(H4’)
,
(H5’)
,
(H6’)
and
(H7’)
hold. Then, the following holds:

1.

Existence
. There exists
c^{\star}>0
and
\mathbf{U}\in\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{R}})\cap X
,
\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{R}})
as in (
4.29
) and
X
as in (
4.39
), such that
(c^{\star},\mathbf{U})
solves (
4.1
) with conditions at infinity (
4.2
), (
4.3
) and
\mathbf{U}
is a global minimizer of
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in
X
, that is,
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U})=0
. Moreover, for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathbf{U}(t)=\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{U}(t))
, where
\mathfrak{P}
is as in
(H6’)
.

2.

Uniqueness of the speed
. The speed
c^{\star}
is unique in the following sense: if
\overline{c^{\star}}>0
is such that

\inf_{U\in X}\mathbf{E}_{\overline{c^{\star}}}(U)=0

(4.41)

and there exists
\overline{\mathbf{U}}\in\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{R}})\cap X
such that
(\overline{c^{\star}},\overline{\mathbf{U}})
solves (
4.1
) and
\mathbf{E}_{\overline{c^{\star}}}(\overline{\mathbf{U}})<+\infty
, then
\overline{c^{\star}}=c^{\star}
.

3.

Exponential convergence
. There exists a constant
M^{+}>0
such that for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\lVert\mathbf{U}(t)-\mathbf{v}^{+}(\mathbf{U})\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq M^{+}e^{-ct},

(4.42)

for some
\mathbf{v}^{+}(\mathbf{U})\in\mathscr{F}^{+}
.

Remark 4.1
.

Given the definition of
X
in (
4.39
), we have that for any
U\in X
and
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
it holds
U(\cdot+\tau)\in X
and for any
c>0
it holds
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U(\cdot+\tau))=e^{-c\tau}\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)
. Such a thing implies

\forall c>0,\hskip 5.69054pt\inf_{U\in X}\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)\in\{-\infty,0\}.

(4.43)

Moreover, we see that in case
c>0
is such that
\inf_{U\in X}\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)=0
one can find plenty of examples of minimizing sequences in
X
which cannot ever reasonably produce a global minimizer. Indeed, consider any function
\tilde{U}\in X
such that
E_{c}(\tilde{U})>0
and then take the minimizing sequence
(\tilde{U}(\cdot+n))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

A more general statement can be given about the uniqueness of the speed, which in particular works for eventual non-minimizing solutions. See Proposition
5.3
.

Theorem
5
will be shown to contain Theorem
1
in section
6
. Notice that, as before, the conditions at infinity (
4.2
) are rather weak (and not really of heteroclinic type), since we do not have convergence to an element of
\mathscr{F}^{-}
as
t\to-\infty
. It is however clear that the conditions at infinity (
4.2
), (
4.3
) are enough to ensure that the solution given by Theorem
5
is not constant. In any case, we can impose an additional assumption in order to obtain stronger conditions at
-\infty
on the solution:

(H8’)
.

Hypothesis
(H7’)
is fulfilled and, additionally:

-a<\frac{(d_{0}\gamma^{-})^{2}}{2},

(4.44)

where
d_{0}
and
\gamma^{-}
were defined in (
4.36
) and (
4.35
) respectively.

Then we can show the following exponential convergence result

Theorem 6
.

Assume that
(H2’)
,
(H3’)
,
(H4’)
,
(H5’)
,
(H6’)
,
(H7’)
and
(H8’)
hold. Then, if
(c^{\star},\mathbf{U})
is the solution given by Theorem
5
, it holds that
\gamma^{-}>c^{\star}
(
\gamma^{-}
as in (
4.35
)) and there exists
\mathfrak{M}^{-}>0
such that for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}

\lVert\mathbf{U}(t)-\mathbf{v}^{-}(\mathbf{U})\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq M^{-}e^{(\gamma^{-}-c^{\star})t}

(4.45)

for some
\mathbf{v}^{-}(U)\in\mathscr{F}^{-}
.

Theorem
6
corresponds to Theorem
2
. Finally, we will prove the following result:

Theorem 7
.

Assume that
(H2’)
,
(H3’)
,
(H4’)
,
(H5’)
,
(H6’)
,
(H7’)
and
(H8’)
hold. Let
(c^{\star},\mathbf{U})
be the solution given by Theorem
5
. Then, if
\tilde{\mathbf{U}}\in\mathcal{A}({\mathbb{R}})\cap X
is such that

\mathbf{E}_{c^{\star}}(\tilde{\mathbf{U}})=0

(4.46)

then we have that
(c^{\star},\tilde{\mathbf{U}})
solves (
4.1
) and

c^{\star}=\frac{-a}{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}dt}.

(4.47)

In particular, the quantity
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert\tilde{\mathbf{U}}^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}dt
is finite. Moreover, we have

c^{\star}=\sup\{c>0:\inf_{U\in X}\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)=-\infty\}=\inf\{c>0:\inf_{U\in X}\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)=0\}

(4.48)

as well as the bound

c^{\star}\leq\frac{\sqrt{-2a}}{d_{0}}<\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{2\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}}}{d_{0}},\gamma^{-}\right\}

(4.49)

with
\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}
as in (
4.33
),
d_{0}
as in (
4.36
) and
\gamma^{-}
as in (
4.35
). The second inequality follows from the bounds on
-a
given by
(H7’)
and
(H8’)
.

Theorem
7
corresponds to Theorem
4
.

5
Proof of the abstract results

5.1
Scheme of the proofs

As pointed out several times, the structure of the proofs of our abstract results, Theorems
5
,
6
and
7
, is analogous to that in Alikakos and Katzourakis
[
7
]
, which has its roots in Alikakos and Fusco
[
5
]
. In fact, most of their results also carry into the abstract setting with the suitable modifications. In fact, the structure of our proofs should be rather compared with
subsection 2.6
in the book by Alikakos, Fusco and Smyrnelis
[
6
]
, which slightly modifies and simplifies the argument in
[
7
]
. We will also rely on some arguments provided in Smyrnelis
[
69
]
, when an analogous abstract approach is taken for the stationary problem. As usual, most of the intermediate results we prove hold under smaller subsets of assumptions (with respect to the set of all assumptions that we dropped in the previous section). Therefore, for the sake of clarity and generality, the necessary assumptions (and only these) that we use to prove a result are specified in its statement.

Despite the previous facts, and as pointed before, several important difficulties not present in
[
7
]
arise when one tries to tackle the same problem in the abstract setting we introduced in the previous section. One of those extra difficulties is due to the fact that, in our setting, we need deal with two different norms in the configuration space of the curves,
\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{H}
(to be thought as
L^{2}
and
H^{1}
respectively, for simplification) and that the potential
\mathcal{E}
is only lower semicontinuous with respect to
\mathscr{L}
-convergence. An additional difficulty comes from the fact that, due to the requirements of our original problem, we are not looking at curves that join two
isolated minimum points
, but rather two
isolated minimum sets
. This turns out to be an obstacle when one tries to adapt argument in
[
7
]
, even if one were to restrict to finite-dimensional configuration spaces. However, this difficulty is successfully dealt with using the precise knowledge about the
projection mappings
(namely assumptions
(H1’)
,
(H4’)
and
(H5’)
) is available. That is, one uses that, for a suitable neighborhood of the minimum sets, the projection onto the sets (both with the
\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{H}
norms) is well defined and enjoys some type of continuity and differentiability properties. This idea, in the Allen-Cahn systems setting, has to be be traced back to Schatzman
[
67
]
.

We will now briefly sketch the scheme of the proof of Theorem
5
. Recall that, according to Remark
4.1
, direct minimization of
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in
X
cannot yield solutions to the problem, the reason being the action of the group of translations. The spaces
X_{T}
, which were introduced in
[
7
]
(also in
[
5
]
for the equal-depth case) and will be precisely presented in (
5.3
), are defined in order to overcome this source of degeneracy, as they are no longer invariant by the action of the group of translations. See the design in Figure
5.1
. As a consequence, compactness is restored and the corresponding minimization problem has a solution for all
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. See Lemma
5.7
later on. In general, minimizers in
X_{T}
solve the profile equation on a (possibly proper) subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
(see Lemma
5.8
), meaning that they are in general not solutions of (
4.1
). However, such constrained minimizers are in fact solutions of (
4.1
) in the case they do not saturate the constraints. Therefore, the goal will be to show the existence of the speed
c^{\star}
such that, for some
T\geq 1
, there exists a constrained minimizer in
X_{T}
which does not saturate the constraints. For that purpose, a careful analysis of the behavior of the constrained minimizers is needed. Indeed, one needs a uniform bound (independent on
T
and continuous on
c
) on the distance between the
entry times
, i. e. the times in which the constrained minimizers enter
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}/2}
. In the balanced case this follows from the fact that the energy density is bounded below by a positive constant outside
\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2}\cup\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2}
(see for instance Smyrnelis
[
69
]
). However this is no longer true for our unbalanced problem, which makes it more involved: If one does not have the positivity of the energy density, the constraint solutions can oscillate between the regions
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}/2}
(producing energy compensations) in larger and larger intervals as
T\to\infty
, so that no
T
-independent bound can be found. This is the main new difficulty with respect to the balanced setting, as one needs new ideas in order to obtain a uniform bound on the distance between the entry times. Our assumption
(H7’)
provides this control because the energy density of the constrained minimizers is bounded below by a positive constant in the interval given by the two entry times mentioned before, meaning that we can argue as in the balanced case. The precise result is Corollary
5.1
. This is the main step in which our proof differs with that in
[
7
]
.

The natural question is what happens if we remove
(H7’)
. A natural approach is to replace
(H7’)
by an assumption more closely related to the one used in
[
7
]
and
[
6
]
. This would lead to introduce a convexity assumption on the level sets of
\mathcal{E}
, as well as some sort of strict monotonicity on well-chosen segments. While this assumption can be worked out in the abstract setting and it is applicable for the finite-dimensional situation considered in
[
7
]
(as we show in the companion paper
[
59
]
), we believe it to be too restrictive to be applied to our original problem.

In any case, after the uniform bound on the entry times of constrained minimizers is obtained, one needs to find the speed
c^{\star}
as, until this point, the speed
c>0
has been only considered as a parameter of the problem without any special role. Our arguments adapts without major difficulty from
[
6
]
and it goes as follows: One introduces a set which classifies the speeds according to the value of the infimum of the corresponding energy on
X
(which, due to the weight and the invariance by translations, is either
-\infty
or
0
). Such a set is
\mathcal{C}
, defined in (
5.169
). Subsequently, one shows (Lemma
5.11
) that
\mathcal{C}
is open, bounded, non-empty and that its positive limit points give rise to entire minimizing solutions of the equations (since for those points one can find corresponding constrained minimizers which do not saturate the constraints). The speed
c^{\star}
is then defined as the supremum of
\mathcal{C}
, which is in fact the unique positive limit point of the set, as shown in Corollary
5.3
. At this point, the process of the proof of Theorem
5
is completed. Later on, we show that the asymptotic behavior of the constrained solutions can be improved under an additional assumption, namely an upper bound on the speed. This is Proposition
5.4
. Theorems
6
and
7
can be then proven.

Figure 5.1:
One-dimensional representation of
X_{T}
. The blue line represents a function
U
belonging to
X_{T}
. The red lines contain the points which are at
\mathscr{L}
-distance smaller than
r_{0}^{\pm}/2
from
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
.

5.2
Preliminaries

Let
r_{0}^{-}
and
r_{0}^{+}
be the constants introduced in section
4
and
\mathscr{F}_{r_{0}^{\pm}/2}^{\pm}
be the corresponding closed balls as in (
4.4
). Assume that
(H1’)
holds. For
T\geq 1
, we define the sets

X_{T}^{-}:=\left\{U\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L}):\forall t\leq-T,\hskip 2.84526ptU(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2}\right\},

(5.1)

X_{T}^{+}:=\left\{U\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L}):\forall t\geq T,\hskip 2.84526ptU(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2}\right\}.

(5.2)

Subsequently, we set

X_{T}:=X_{T}^{-}\cap X_{T}^{+}.

(5.3)

Recall the space
X
introduced in (
4.39
). Notice that

X=\bigcup_{T\geq 1}X_{T}.

(5.4)

We have the following preliminary properties on the spaces
X_{T}
:

Lemma 5.1
.

Assume that
(H1’)
holds. Let
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. For any
U\in X_{T}
, we have that

\forall t\geq T,\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(U(t))\geq 0.

(5.5)

Moreover, the quantity
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)
as introduced in (
4.23
) is well defined in
(-\infty,+\infty]
.

Proof.

Let
U\in X_{T}
. Notice that for
t\geq T
, we have that
U(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}/2}
. Therefore, (
5.5
) follows directly from (
4.11
) in Lemma
4.1
.

Let now
\mathcal{E}^{+}(U)\geq 0
and
\mathcal{E}^{-}(U)\geq 0
be, respectively, the non-negative and the non-positive part of
\mathcal{E}(U)
, so that
\mathcal{E}(U)=\mathcal{E}^{+}(U)-\mathcal{E}^{-}(U)
. We have that
\mathcal{E}^{-}(U)
is null on
[T,+\infty)
. That is

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathcal{E}^{-}(U(t))e^{ct}dt=\int_{-\infty}^{T}\mathcal{E}^{-}(U(t))e^{ct}dt\leq-\frac{a}{c}e^{cT}<+\infty,

(5.6)

where
a
is the minimum value from
(H1’)
. Therefore, the negative part of the energy density
\mathbf{e}_{c}(U)
(see (
4.23
)) belongs to
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
, which establishes the result.
∎

Lemma
5.1
shows that for any
T\geq 1
and
c>0
,
\mathbf{E}_{c}
is well defined as an extended functional on
X_{T}
, at least if sufficient hypothesis are made. Moreover, it gives the following useful inequalities:

Lemma 5.2
.

Assume that
(H1’)
holds. Let
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. For any
U\in X_{T}
, we have that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{\lVert U^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{2}e^{ct}dt\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)-\frac{a}{c}e^{cT}

(5.7)

and

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\lvert\mathcal{E}(U(t))\rvert e^{ct}dt\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)-\frac{a}{c}e^{cT}.

(5.8)

Finally, we have that for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\int_{t}^{+\infty}\lVert U^{\prime}(s)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}ds\leq\left(\left(\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)-\frac{a}{c}e^{cT}\right)\frac{e^{-ct}}{c}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(5.9)

Proof.

Using (
5.5
) in Lemma
5.1
, we get that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{\lVert U^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{2}e^{ct}dt\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)-\int_{-\infty}^{T}\mathcal{E}(U(t))e^{ct},

(5.10)

which, by
(H1’)
, implies that (
5.7
) holds. Inequality (
5.8
) is obtained in the same fashion. Finally, we have that (
5.9
) follows by combining (
5.7
) with Cauchy-Schwartz inequality.
∎

The previous results allow to prove the following convergence properties at
+\infty
for finite energy functions in
X_{T}
:

Lemma 5.3
.

Assume that
(H1’)
and
(H5’)
hold. Let
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. Take
U\in X_{T}
such that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)<+\infty
. Then, we have that there exists a subsequence
(t_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
{\mathbb{R}}
such that
t_{n}\to+\infty
as
n\to\infty
and

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}(U(t_{n}))e^{ct_{n}}=0.

(5.11)

Moreover, there exists
\mathbf{v}^{+}(U)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}
such that for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
it holds

\lVert U(t)-\mathbf{v}^{+}(U)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}\leq\left(\frac{\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)-\frac{a}{c}e^{cT}}{c}\right)e^{-ct}.

(5.12)

That is,
U
tends to
\mathbf{v}^{+}(U)
at
+\infty
with an exponential rate of convergence and with respect to the
\mathscr{L}
-norm.

Proof.

We have by (
5.8
) in Lemma
5.2
that
t\in{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{E}(U(t))e^{ct}\in{\mathbb{R}}
belongs to
L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
because
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)<+\infty
. Therefore, combining with (
5.5
) in Lemma
4.1
, we obtain (
5.11
).

Subsequently, notice that (
5.9
) in Lemma
5.2
and the fact that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)<+\infty
gives the existence of
\mathbf{v}^{+}(U)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}
such that
\lim_{t\to+\infty}\lVert U(t)-\mathbf{v}^{+}(U)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}=0
. Therefore, fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and notice that for any
\tilde{t}>t
we have

\lVert U(\tilde{t})-U(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\int_{t}^{\tilde{t}}\lVert U^{\prime}(s)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}ds\leq\int_{t}^{+\infty}\lVert U^{\prime}(s)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}},

(5.13)

which by (
5.9
) in Lemma
5.2
means that

\lVert U(\tilde{t})-U(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}\leq\left(\frac{\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)-\frac{a}{c}e^{cT}}{c}\right)e^{-ct}.

(5.14)

Therefore, passing to the limit
\tilde{t}\to+\infty
we obtain (
5.12
), also due to the fact that
U
is continuous with respect to the
\mathscr{L}
-norm.

∎

Remark 5.1
.

Notice that (
5.12
) in Lemma
5.3
does not imply convergence of
\mathcal{E}(U)
towards 0 at
+\infty
, due to the fact that
\mathcal{E}
is not continuous with respect to the
\mathscr{L}
norm.

Remark 5.2
.

Regarding the behavior at
-\infty
, notice that we can only say that if
U\in X_{T}
is such that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)<+\infty
, then
\mathcal{E}(U)
does not go to
+\infty
faster than
e^{ct}
at the limit
t\to-\infty
. That is, almost nothing can be said for generic finite energy solutions regarding their behavior at
-\infty
.

5.3
The infima of
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in
X_{T}
are well defined

Once we have defined the spaces
X_{T}
, we show that the corresponding infimum of
\mathbf{E}_{c}
is well defined as a real number for all
c>0
. Set

\mathbf{m}_{c,T}:=\inf_{U\in X_{T}}\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)\in[-\infty,+\infty).

(5.15)

We have the following:

Lemma 5.4
.

Assume that
(H1’)
and
(H2’)
hold. Fix
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{\pm}\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
. Let
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. For all
T\geq 1
the function

\Psi(t):=\begin{cases}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{-}&\mbox{ if }t\leq-1,\\
\frac{1-t}{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{-}+\frac{t+1}{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{+}&\mbox{ if }-1\leq t\leq 1,\\
\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{+}&\mbox{ if }t\geq 1,\end{cases}

(5.16)

belongs to
X_{T}
. Moreover, for all
c>0

\mathbf{E}_{c}(\Psi)<+\infty.

(5.17)

Furthermore, we have

-\infty<\mathbf{m}_{c,T}<+\infty.

(5.18)

Proof.

It is clear that
\Psi\in X_{T}
. We now show that (
5.17
) holds. Notice first that

\int_{-\infty}^{1}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\Psi)=\int_{-\infty}^{1}ae^{ct}dt=\frac{a}{c}e^{c},

(5.19)

where
a
is the minimum value from
(H1’)
. Subsequently, we have

\int_{1}^{+\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\Psi)=0

(5.20)

and

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\Psi)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\left[\frac{\left\lVert\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{+}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{-}\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{8}+\mathcal{E}\left(\frac{1-t}{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{-}+\frac{t+1}{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{+}\right)\right]e^{ct}dt

(5.21)

\displaystyle\leq\left[\frac{\left\lVert\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{+}-\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{-}\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{4}+2\max_{t\in[-1,1]}\mathcal{E}\left(\frac{1-t}{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{-}+\frac{t+1}{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{+}\right)\right]\frac{e^{c}-e^{-c}}{c}

(5.22)

and we have

\max_{t\in[-1,1]}\mathcal{E}\left(\frac{1-t}{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{-}+\frac{t+1}{2}\hat{\mathbf{v}}^{+}\right)<+\infty,

(5.23)

by
(H2’)
. Therefore, we have obtained
E_{c}(\Psi)<+\infty
, which readily implies that
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}<+\infty
. In order to establish (
5.18
), we still need to show that
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}>-\infty
. For that purpose, let
U\in X_{T}
. By (
5.5
) in Lemma
5.1
, we have

\int_{T}^{+\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U)\geq 0.

(5.24)

We also have

\int_{-\infty}^{T}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U)\geq\int_{-\infty}^{T}a\mathbf{e}^{ct}dt=\frac{a}{c}e^{cT}.

(5.25)

That is

\forall U\in X_{T},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)\geq\frac{a}{c}e^{cT}>-\infty,

(5.26)

which means that
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}>-\infty
.
∎

The next goal will be to show that, under the proper assumptions, we have that for any
c>0
and
T\geq 1
, the infimum values defined in (
5.15
) are attained. Such a fact is not hard to prove since the constraints that define the spaces
X_{T}
allow to restore compactness. It relies on some properties that will be proven in the next subsection.

5.4
General continuity and semi-continuity results

We now provide some results which address continuity and semicontinuity properties of the energies
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in the spaces
X_{T}
. Such properties will allow us to show that the infimum values defined in (
5.15
) are attained under the proper assumptions. They will be also be useful in a more advanced stage of the proof, when the constrains will be removed. For now, we essentially adapt some results from
[
7
]
to our setting.

Our first result is essentially
Lemma 26
in
[
7
]
:

Lemma 5.5
.

Assume that
(H1’)
holds. Fix
T\geq 1
and
U\in X_{T}
. Consider the set

A_{T,U}:=\{c>0:\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)<+\infty\}.

(5.27)

Then, if
c\in A_{T,U}
, then
(0,c]\subset A_{T,U}
. Moreover, the correspondence

c\in A_{T,U}\to\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)\in{\mathbb{R}}

(5.28)

is continuous.

Proof.

Let
c\in A_{T,U}
. On the one hand, inequality (
5.5
) in Lemma
5.1
gives

0\leq\int_{T}^{+\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)-\int_{-\infty}^{T}\mathcal{E}^{-}(U(t))e^{ct}dt\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)-ae^{cT}<+\infty,

(5.29)

which implies that a. e. in
(T,+\infty)

0\leq\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(\cdot))\in L^{1}((T,+\infty)).

(5.30)

Therefore, if
c^{\prime}\leq c
we have that a. e. in
(T,+\infty)

0\leq\mathbf{e}_{c^{\prime}}(U(\cdot))\leq\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(\cdot))\in L^{1}(T,+\infty).

(5.31)

On the other hand,

\int_{-\infty}^{T}\lvert\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))\rvert dt\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)+2ae^{cT}<+\infty

(5.32)

because
\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(\cdot))
is non-negative a. e. in
[T,+\infty)
. The previous inequality shows

\lvert\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(\cdot))\rvert\in L^{1}((-\infty,T)),

(5.33)

and we have that a. e. in
(-\infty,T)

\lvert\mathbf{e}_{c^{\prime}}(U(\cdot))\rvert\leq\lvert\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(\cdot))\rvert\in L^{1}((-\infty,T)).

(5.34)

Combining (
5.31
) and (
5.34
), we obtain that
\lvert e_{c}(U(\cdot))\rvert\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}})
, meaning that
c^{\prime}\in A_{T,U}
. Hence, we have
(0,c]\subset A_{T,U}
as we wanted to show.

Consider now a sequence
(c_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
A_{T,U}
such that
c_{n}\to c_{\infty}\in A_{T,U}
. The sequence
(c_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is convergent (so in particular it is bounded), meaning that in case it does not attain its
\sup
we must have
c_{\infty}=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}c_{n}
. Therefore, we can set

\hat{c}:=\begin{cases}c_{\infty}&\mbox{ if }c_{\infty}=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}c_{n},\\
\max_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}c_{n}&\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}

(5.35)

and we obviously have
\hat{c}\in A_{T,U}
. As a consequence, (
5.31
) and (
5.34
) imply that a. e. in
{\mathbb{R}}

\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}},\hskip 5.69054pt\lvert\mathbf{e}_{c_{n}}(U(\cdot))\rvert\leq\lvert\mathbf{e}_{\hat{c}}(U(\cdot))\rvert\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}).

(5.36)

Since we also have
\mathbf{e}_{c_{n}}(U(\cdot))\to\mathbf{e}_{c_{\infty}}(U(\cdot))
pointwise a. e. in
{\mathbb{R}}
, the Dominated Convergence Theorem gives the result.
∎

We now show a lower semicontinuity result, which in particular will imply the existence of the constrained solutions:

Lemma 5.6
.

Assume that
(H1’)
,
(H3’)
and
(H4’)
hold. Let
T\geq 1
be fixed. Let
(U_{n}^{i})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence in
X_{T}
and
(c_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
a convergent sequence of positive real numbers such that

\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbf{E}_{c_{n}}(U_{n}^{i})<+\infty.

(5.37)

Then, there exists a sequence
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
X_{T}
and
U_{\infty}\in X_{T}
such that up to extracting a subsequence in
(U_{n},c_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
it holds

\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathbf{E}_{c_{n}}(U_{n})=\mathbf{E}_{c_{n}}(U_{n}^{i}),

(5.38)

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\hskip 5.69054ptU_{n}(t)\rightharpoonup U_{\infty}(t)\mbox{ weakly in }\mathscr{L}

(5.39)

U_{n}^{\prime}e^{c_{n}\mathrm{Id}/2}\rightharpoonup U_{\infty}^{\prime}e^{c_{\infty}\mathrm{Id}/2}\mbox{ weakly in }L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L})

(5.40)

and

\mathbf{E}_{c_{\infty}}(U_{\infty})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{c_{n}}(U_{n}),

(5.41)

where
c_{\infty}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}c_{n}
.

Proof.

Denote
M:=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}E_{c_{n}}(U_{n}^{i})
, which is finite by (
5.37
). We will now use
(H4’)
. We assume that 2. holds, the argument when 1. holds being similar and easier. Fix any
\mathbf{v}^{+}\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
and for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, set
v_{n}:=U_{n}^{i}(T)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}
. Define

U_{n}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\to\hat{P}_{(v_{n},\mathbf{v}^{+})}(U^{i}_{n}(t)),

(5.42)

where
\hat{P}_{\mathbf{v}^{+}}
is the differentiable operator introduced in
(H4’)
. We apply the properties summarized in 2. of
(H4’)
. Notice that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have
U_{n}\in X_{T}
due to (
4.15
). The energy equality (
5.38
) follows from (
4.16
) and (
4.17
). Moreover, (
4.14
) implies that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

P^{+}(U_{n}(T))=P^{+}(\hat{P}_{(v_{n},\mathbf{v}^{+})}(U^{i}_{n}(T)))=P^{+}(\hat{P}_{(v_{n},\mathbf{v}^{+})}(v_{n}))=\mathbf{v}^{+},

(5.43)

which in particular means

\lVert U_{n}(T)-\mathbf{v}^{+}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}.

(5.44)

Notice now that
\mathcal{E}(U(\cdot))
is non-negative in
[T,+\infty)
as
U\in X_{T}
by (
5.5
) in Lemma
5.1
, therefore

\displaystyle\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}},\hskip 5.69054pt\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert U^{\prime}_{n}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}e^{c_{n}t}dt

\displaystyle\leq M-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t))e^{c_{n}t}dt

(5.45)

\displaystyle\leq M-\int_{-\infty}^{T}\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t))e^{c_{n}t}dt\leq\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left\{M-\frac{a}{c_{n}}e^{c_{n}T}\right\}<+\infty.

(5.46)

That is, we have that
(U_{n}^{\prime}e^{c_{n}\mathrm{Id}/2})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L})
. Therefore, there exists
\tilde{U}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L})
such that

U_{n}^{\prime}e^{c_{n}\mathrm{Id}/2}\rightharpoonup\tilde{U}\mbox{ weakly in }L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L})

(5.47)

up to subsequences. Such a thing implies

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert\tilde{U}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}dt\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert U^{\prime}_{n}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}e^{c_{n}t}dt.

(5.48)

Now, notice that by (
5.44
) we have that
(U_{n}(T))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is bounded in
\mathscr{L}
. Therefore, up to an extraction there exists
v_{\infty}\in\mathscr{L}
such that

U_{n}(T)\rightharpoonup v_{\infty}\mbox{ in }\mathscr{L}.

(5.49)

As in
[
69
]
, we point out that

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}},\hskip 5.69054ptU_{n}(t)=U_{n}(T)+\int_{T}^{t}U_{n}^{\prime}(s)ds.

(5.50)

Now, notice that for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have
\mathbf{1}_{(0,t)}e^{-c_{n}\mathrm{Id}/2}\to\mathbf{1}_{(0,t)}e^{-c_{\infty}\mathrm{Id}/2}
in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
, where
\mathbf{1}
states for the indicator function of a set. Therefore, we obtain by (
5.47
) and (
5.49
)

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\hskip 5.69054ptU_{n}(t)\rightharpoonup U_{\infty}(t):=v_{\infty}+\int_{T}^{t}\tilde{U}(s)e^{-c_{\infty}s/2}ds,

(5.51)

which gives (
5.39
). Moreover, we have that
U_{\infty}\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L})
and
U_{\infty}^{\prime}=\tilde{U}e^{-c_{\infty}\mathrm{Id}/2}
, meaning by (
5.47
) that (
5.40
) also holds.

Recall now that
\mathcal{E}
is lower semicontinuous on
\mathscr{L}
by
(H1’)
, so that (
5.39
) gives

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(U_{\infty}(t))\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t)).

(5.52)

We need to show that
U_{\infty}\in X_{T}
and to establish the inequality (
5.41
).

•

We begin by showing that
U_{\infty}\in X_{T}
. We need to show that for all
t\in[T,+\infty)
, it holds
U_{\infty}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2}
and similarly for
(-\infty,-T]
. Fix
t\in[T,+\infty)
. We have that
U_{n}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2}
, so we can define the sequence
(\mathbf{v}^{+}_{n}(t))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
\mathscr{F}^{+}
as
\mathbf{v}^{+}_{n}(t):=P^{+}(U_{n}(t))
. We show that such a sequence is bounded. Indeed, we have

\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}},\hskip 5.69054pt\lVert\mathbf{v}_{n}^{+}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}+\lVert U_{n}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}

(5.53)

and
(U_{n}(t))_{\mathscr{L}}
converges weakly in
\mathscr{L}
, so in particular it is bounded. Therefore, up to an extraction we can assume that
\mathbf{v}_{n}^{+}(t)\rightharpoonup\mathbf{v}^{+}_{\infty}(t)\in\mathscr{L}
and by
(H3’)
we have
\mathbf{v}^{+}_{\infty}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
. Using now the convergence properties we get the inequality

\lVert U_{\infty}(t)-\mathbf{v}^{+}_{\infty}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\lVert U_{n}(t)-\mathbf{v}_{n}^{+}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2},

(5.54)

so that
U_{n}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2}
. An identical argument shows that for all
t\in(-\infty,-T]
we have
U_{n}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2}
. Therefore, we have shown that
U_{\infty}\in X_{T}
.

•

Next, we prove (
5.41
). We have

\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t))e^{c_{n}t}dt\leq M-\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lVert U_{n}^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{2}e^{ct}dt<+\infty,

(5.55)

by (
5.45
). Hence, we can apply Fatou’s Lemma to
(t\in{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t))e^{c_{n}t})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
(a sequence of functions uniformly bounded below by
a
) to show

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\liminf_{n\to+\infty}\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t))e^{c_{n}t}dt\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t))e^{c_{n}t}dt,

(5.56)

which, combined with (
5.52
) implies

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{E}(U_{\infty}(t))e^{ct}dt\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t))e^{c_{n}t}dt.

(5.57)

Combining (
5.48
) and (
5.57
) we get

\mathbf{E}_{c}(U_{\infty})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\lVert U^{\prime}_{n}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{2}e^{c_{n}t}dt+\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{E}(U_{n}(t))e^{c_{n}t}dt,

(5.58)

which, by superadditivity of the limit inferior gives (
5.41
).

∎

5.5
Existence of an infimum for
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in
X_{T}

The goal now is to show that, for
T\geq 1
and
c>0
fixed, the infimum
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}
as defined in (
5.15
) is attained by a function in
X_{T}
. This will actually follow easily from Lemma
5.6
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Assume that
(H1’)
,
(H2’)
,
(H3’)
and
(H4’)
hold. Let
c>0
,
T\geq 1
and
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}
be as in (
5.15
). Then,
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}
is attained for some
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in X_{T}
.

Proof.

By (
5.18
) in Lemma
5.4
, we have that there exists a minimizing sequence
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
for
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in
X_{T}
. We apply Lemma
5.6
to
(U_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and the sequence of speeds constantly equal to
c
. We obtain a function
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in X_{T}
such that

\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{E}_{c}(U_{n})=\mathbf{m}_{c,T},

(5.59)

due to (
5.41
). Therefore,
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}
is attained by
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}
in
X_{T}
.
∎

Subsequently, we show that assumption
(H6’)
implies that the constrained minimizers are solutions of the equation in a certain set containing
(-T,T)
, with the proper regularity.

Lemma 5.8
.

Assume that
(H6’)
holds. Let
c>0
,
T\geq 1
and
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}
be as in (
5.15
). Let
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in X_{T}
be such that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})=\mathbf{m}_{c,T}
. Then,
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in\mathcal{A}((-T,T))
,
\mathcal{A}((-T,T))
as in (
4.29
) and

\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime\prime}-D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})=-c\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime}\mbox{ in }(-T,T).

(5.60)

Moreover, if
t\geq T
is such that

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t),\mathscr{F}^{+})<\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2},

(5.61)

then, there exists
\delta^{+}(t)>0
such that
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in\mathcal{A}((t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)))
and

\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime\prime}-D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})=-c\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime}\mbox{ in }(t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)).

(5.62)

Similarly, if
t\leq-T
is such that

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})<\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2},

(5.63)

then, there exists
\delta^{-}(t)>0
such that
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in\mathcal{A}((t-\delta^{-}(t),t+\delta^{-}(t)))
and

\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime\prime}-D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})=-c\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime}\mbox{ in }(t-\delta^{-}(t),t+\delta^{-}(t)).

(5.64)

Proof.

We first show that

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t))=\mathbf{U}_{c.T}(t),

(5.65)

where
\mathfrak{P}
is the map from
(H6’)
. We claim that the function

\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}}:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t))

(5.66)

belongs to
X_{T}
. Indeed, this follows from (
4.27
))and (
4.28
). Property (
4.26
) implies that

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}}(t))\leq\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t)).

(5.67)

Take now
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
s\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{t\}
. Property (
4.27
) implies that

\left\lVert\frac{\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}}(t)-\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}}(s)}{t-s}\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\left\lVert\frac{\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t)-\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(s)}{t-s}\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}},

(5.68)

which, by Lebesgue’s differentiation Theorem implies that

\mbox{for a. e. }t\in{\mathbb{R}},\hskip 5.69054pt\lVert(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}})^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\leq\lVert\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}.

(5.69)

By contradiction, assume now that there exists
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t)\not=\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t))=\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}}(t)
. Property (
4.27
) implies that
\mathfrak{P}
is a
\mathscr{L}
-continuous map. Therefore, since
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}
is also
\mathscr{L}
-continuous, we must have that for some non-empty interval
I_{t}\ni t
, it holds

\forall s\in I_{t},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(s)\not=\mathfrak{P}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(s))=\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}}(s),

(5.70)

so that, using (
4.26
) we get

\forall s\in I_{t},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}}(s))<\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(s))

(5.71)

so that, combining with (
5.67
) and (
5.69
) we obtain

\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}})<\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})=\mathbf{m}_{c,T},

(5.72)

which contradicts the definition of
\mathbf{m}_{c,T}
(
5.15
) since
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\mathfrak{P}}\in X_{T}
. Therefore, we have shown that (
5.65
) holds. Next, notice that

\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T};[-T,T])\leq\mathbf{m}_{c,T}-\frac{a}{c}e^{-cT}<+\infty

(5.73)

and for any
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}((-T,T),(\mathscr{H},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}))
we have
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}+\phi\in X_{T}
, so that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})\leq\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}+\phi)
. Therefore, the restriction of
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}
in
(-T,T)
is a local minimizer of
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\cdot,[-T,T])
in the sense of Definition
4.1
. Since
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}
also verifies (
5.65
), we can apply the regularity assumption
(H6’)
. Therefore,
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in\mathcal{A}((-T,T))
and (
5.60
) holds. Assume now that there exists
t\geq T
such that (
5.61
) holds. Then, there exists
\mathbf{v}^{+}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}
such that

\lVert\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t)-\mathbf{v}^{+}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}<\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}

(5.74)

which, since
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}
is
\mathscr{L}
-continuous, implies that there exists
\delta^{+}(t)>0
such that

\forall s\in(t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)),\hskip 5.69054pt\lVert\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(s)-\mathbf{v}^{+}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}<\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}-d^{+}(t)

(5.75)

where

d^{+}(t):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}-\lVert\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t)-\mathbf{v}^{+}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}\right)>0.

(5.76)

Therefore, if
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}((t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)),(\mathscr{H},\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}))
is such that

\max_{t\in[t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)]}\lVert\phi(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}\leq\frac{d^{+}(t)}{2}

(5.77)

we have that

\forall s\in(t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)),\hskip 5.69054pt\lVert\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(s)+\phi(s)-\mathbf{v}^{+}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}<\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}-\frac{d^{+}(t)}{2}

(5.78)

so that
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}+\phi\in X_{T}
. Meaning that
\mathbf{E}_{c,T}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})\leq\mathbf{E}_{c,T}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}+\phi)
. Since
\phi
is supported on
[t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)]
, the previous implies that

\mathbf{E}_{c,T}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T};[t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)])\leq\mathbf{E}_{c,T}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}+\phi;[t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)]),

(5.79)

so that
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}
is a local minimizer of
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\cdot;[t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)])
in the sense of Definition
4.1
. Since (
5.65
) also holds, we can apply
(H6’)
and obtain that
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in\mathcal{A}((t-\delta^{+}(t),t+\delta^{+}(t)))
and equation (
5.62
) holds. If
t\leq-T
is such that (
5.63
) holds, the same reasoning shows that for some
\delta^{-}(t)>0
,
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in\mathcal{A}((t-\delta^{-}(t),t+\delta^{-}(t)))
and (
5.61
) holds, which concludes the proof of the result.
∎

5.6
The comparison result

The goal of this subsection is to obtain relevant information on the behavior of the constrained minimizers. Such information is contained in Corollary
5.1
and it will allow us to remove the constraints later on. In order to carry on these arguments, assumption
(H7’)
will become necessary since it will show that our problem can be somehow dealt with as in the balanced one, which will allow us to argue in a fashion similar to Smyrnelis
[
69
]
. We begin by introducing some constants. For
0<r\leq r_{0}^{\pm}
, recall the definition of
\kappa_{r}^{\pm}
introduced in (
4.10
), Lemma
4.1
. We define

\eta^{+}_{0}:=\min\left\{\sqrt{e^{-1}\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{4}\sqrt{2\kappa^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/4}}},\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{4}\right\}>0,

(5.80)

\hat{r}^{+}:=\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{C^{+}+1}>0,

(5.81)

\mathcal{E}^{+}_{\max}:=\frac{1}{\left(C^{+}\right)^{2}(C^{+}+1)}\min\left\{\frac{(\eta_{0}^{+})^{2}}{4},\kappa^{+}_{\eta_{0}^{+}},\beta^{+}(\hat{r}^{+}),\beta^{+}(\eta_{0}^{+})\right\}>0,

(5.82)

where the constants
C^{\pm}
,
\beta^{\pm}(\hat{r}^{\pm}),\beta(\eta_{0}^{\pm})
were introduced in
(H5’)
. Recall that in (
4.31
), (
4.32
), (
4.33
) we introduced the analogous constants

\eta_{0}^{-}:=\min\left\{\sqrt{e^{-1}\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{4}\sqrt{2(\kappa^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/4}-a)}},\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{4}\right\}>0,

(5.83)

\hat{r}^{-}:=\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{C^{-}+1}>0

(5.84)

and

\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}:=\frac{1}{(C^{-})^{2}(C^{-}+1)}\min\left\{\frac{(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}}{4},\kappa_{\eta_{0}^{-}}^{-}-a,\beta^{-}(\hat{r}^{-}),\beta^{-}(\eta_{0}^{-})\right\}>0.

(5.85)

For any
U\in X_{T}
, define

t^{-}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}):=\sup\left\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}:\mathcal{E}(U(t))\leq a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}\mbox{ and }\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})\leq\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}\right\}

(5.86)

and

t^{+}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}):=\inf\left\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}:\mathcal{E}(U(t))\leq\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}\mbox{ and }\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{+})\leq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}\right\}.

(5.87)

We have the following technical property:

Lemma 5.9
.

Assume that
(H1’)
and
(H5’)
hold. Let
\hat{r}^{\pm}>0
be as in (
5.81
), (
5.84
) and
\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{\pm}
be as in (
5.82
), (
5.85
). Then, if
v\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}}
is such that

\mathcal{E}(v)\leq\min\{\pm(-a),0\}+\beta^{\pm}(\hat{r}^{\pm}),

(5.88)

then

\forall\lambda\in[0,1],\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(\lambda v+(1-\lambda)P^{\pm}(v))\leq\min\{\pm(-a),0\}+\mathsf{C}^{\pm}(\mathcal{E}(v)-\min\{\pm(-a),0\}).

(5.89)

In particular, if

\mathcal{E}(u)\leq\min\{\pm(-a),0\}+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{\pm},

(5.90)

then

\forall\lambda\in[0,1],\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(\lambda v+(1-\lambda)P^{\pm}(v))\leq\min\{\pm(-a),0\}+\mathsf{C}^{\pm}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{\pm}.

(5.91)

The constants
\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{\pm}
,
\mathsf{C}^{\pm}
where defined in (
5.85
), (
5.82
) and (
4.34
) respectively.
P^{\pm}(u)
is the projection introduced in
(H1’)
.

Proof.

Assume that (
5.88
) holds for
v\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}}
. Then, invoking
(H5’)
we have that
v\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathscr{H},\hat{r}^{\pm}}
, so in particular the projection
P^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H}}(u)
is well defined. Fix
\lambda\in[0,1]
. Since
v\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}}
, the projection
P^{\pm}(v)
is well defined by
(H1’)
. Using (
4.20
) we obtain

\displaystyle\lVert\lambda v+(1-\lambda)P^{\pm}(v)-P^{\pm}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\lVert v-P^{\pm}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}

(5.92)

\displaystyle\leq(C^{\pm}+1)\lVert v-P^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H}}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}\leq(C^{\pm}+1)\hat{r}^{\pm}

(5.93)

so that
\lambda v+(1-\lambda)P^{\pm}(v)\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H},r_{0}^{\pm}}
by the definition of
\hat{r}^{\pm}
in (
5.81
), (
5.84
). Using now again (
4.20
) along with the estimate (
4.22
) in
(H5’)
, we get

\lVert P^{\pm}(v)-P^{\pm}_{\mathscr{H}}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}\leq(C^{\pm})^{2}(\mathcal{E}(v)-\min\{\pm(-a),0\})

(5.94)

which, plugging into (
5.92
), gives

\displaystyle\lVert\lambda v+(1-\lambda)P^{\pm}(v)-P_{\mathscr{H}}^{\pm}(v)\rVert_{\mathscr{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}((C^{\pm})^{2}+(C^{\pm}+1)^{2})(\mathcal{E}(v)-\min\{\pm(-a),0\}),

(5.95)

that, using again (
4.22
), implies exactly (
5.89
). Assuming now that (
5.90
) holds, we have by (
5.85
), (
5.82
) that in particular (
5.88
) holds. Therefore, (
5.91
) follows from (
5.89
).
∎

Next, we have the following property:

Lemma 5.10
.

Assume that
(H5’)
and
(H7’)
hold. Let
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. Assume that
U\in X_{T}
is such that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)\leq 0
. Then the quantities
t^{-}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-})
and
t^{+}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+})
defined in (
5.86
) and (
5.87
), respectively, are well defined as real numbers. Moreover, it holds that

\mathcal{E}(U(t^{-}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}))\leq a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-},\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t^{-}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-})),\mathscr{F}^{-})\leq\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}

(5.96)

and

\mathcal{E}(U(t^{+}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}))\leq\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+},\hskip 2.84526pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t^{+}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+})),\mathscr{F}^{+})\leq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}.

(5.97)

Proof.

Using that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)\leq 0
and the fact that
\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}:\mathcal{E}(U(t))>0\}
is nonempty since
U\in X_{T}
, we must have that

\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}:\mathcal{E}(U(t))<0\}\not=\emptyset

(5.98)

and if
v\in\mathscr{L}
is such that
\mathcal{E}(v)<0
, then we have
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(v,\mathscr{F}^{-})\leq r_{0}^{-}/2
by (
4.38
) in
(H7’)
and
\mathcal{E}(v)<a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}
since we assume
a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}>0
. Therefore,
t^{-}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-})
is well defined, as we have shown that

\left\{t\in{\mathbb{R}}:\mathcal{E}(U(t))\leq a+\mathcal{E}^{-}_{\max}\mbox{ and }\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})\leq\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}\right\}\not=\emptyset

(5.99)

and such set is bounded above by
T
, because
\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2}\cap\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2}=\emptyset
. Using Lemma
5.3
, we have that
t^{+}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+})
is well defined. Finally, inequalities (
5.96
) and (
5.97
) follow because
t\in{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{E}(U(t))\in{\mathbb{R}}
is lower semicontinuous by
(H1’)
and
t\to\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{\pm})
is continuous whenever
U(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{\pm}_{r_{0}^{\pm}/2}
by
(H1’)
(recall that
t\in{\mathbb{R}}\to U(t)\in\mathscr{L}
is continuous because
U\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathbb{R}},\mathscr{L})
).
∎

The main work is done by the following result:

Proposition 5.1
.

Assume that
(H5’)
and
(H7’)
hold. Let
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. Consider
U\in X_{T}
with
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)\leq 0
. Let
t^{\pm}:=t^{\pm}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{\pm},\eta_{0}^{\pm})
be as in (
5.86
) and (
5.87
). Then,
t^{\pm}
are well defined by Lemma
5.10
. Moreover, if there exists
\tilde{t}^{-}<t^{-}
such that

r_{0}^{-}\geq\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(\tilde{t}^{-}),\mathscr{F}^{-})\geq\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}

(5.100)

then, we can find
\tilde{U}^{-}\in X_{T}
such that

\forall t\leq t^{-},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})<\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2},

(5.101)

and

\mathbf{E}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-})<\mathbf{E}_{c}(U).

(5.102)

Analogously, if there exists
\tilde{t}^{+}>t^{+}
such that

r_{0}^{+}\geq\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(\tilde{t}^{+}),\mathscr{F}^{+})\geq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2},

(5.103)

then we can find
\tilde{U}^{+}\in X_{T}
such that

\forall t\geq t^{+},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\tilde{U}^{+}(t),\mathscr{F}^{+})<\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}

(5.104)

and

\mathbf{E}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{+})<\mathbf{E}_{c}(U).

(5.105)

Furthermore, we have that

0<t^{+}-t^{-}\leq\mathsf{T}_{\star}(c),

(5.106)

where

\mathsf{T}_{\star}(c):=\frac{1}{c}\ln\left(\frac{-a}{\alpha_{\star}}+1\right),

(5.107)

with
\alpha_{\star}>0
a constant which is independent from
c
,
T
and
U
.

The idea of the proof of Proposition
5.1
is pictured in Figure
5.2
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

We begin by proving the first part of the result for
\mathscr{F}^{-}
. Recall that Lemma
5.10
gives

\mathcal{E}(U(t^{-}))\leq a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}.

(5.108)

and
U(t^{-})\in\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2}
. Since
a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}\leq\beta(\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-})
by the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}
, (
5.85
), we have by
(H5’)
that

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t^{-}),\mathscr{F}^{-})\leq\eta_{0}^{-}.

(5.109)

Assume that there exists
\tilde{t}^{-}<t^{-}
such that (
5.100
) is satisfied. Moreover, we assume, as we can, that

\tilde{t}^{-}:=\max\left\{t\leq t^{-}:\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})\geq\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}\right\}

(5.110)

(the
\sup
can be replaced by a
\max
by continuity). Define

t^{-}_{0}:=\inf\{t\in[\tilde{t}^{-},t^{-}]:\mathcal{E}(U(t))\leq a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}\mbox{ and }\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t))\leq\eta_{0}^{-}\}.

(5.111)

Let
\mathbf{v}^{-}:=P^{-}(U(t^{-}_{0}))\in\mathscr{F}^{-}
, with
P^{-}
as in
(H1’)
. Notice that due to (
5.110
), we have

\forall t\in[t^{-}_{0},t^{-}],\hskip 5.69054pt\mathrm{dist}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})<\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}.

(5.112)

The proof now bifurcates according to two possible cases:

•

t^{-}_{0}\leq\tilde{t}^{-}+1
. In that case, set

\tilde{U}^{-}(t):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}^{-}&\mbox{ if }t\leq t_{0}^{-}-1,\\
(t_{0}^{-}-t)\mathbf{v}^{-}+(t-t_{0}^{-}+1)U(t_{0}^{-})&\mbox{ if }t^{-}_{0}-1\leq t\leq t_{0}^{-},\\
U(t)&\mbox{ if }t\geq t^{-}_{0},\end{cases}

(5.113)

which belongs to
X_{T}
. Due to the definition of
\tilde{U}^{-}
and (
5.112
), we have that
\tilde{U}^{-}
satisfies (
5.101
). It remains to check (
5.102
). We have

\int_{t_{0}^{-}-1}^{t_{0}^{-}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt\leq\int_{t_{0}^{-}-1}^{t_{0}^{-}}\left[\frac{\lVert U(t_{0}^{-})-\mathbf{v}^{-}\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{E}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))\right]e^{ct}dt.

(5.114)

Fix
t\in[t^{-}_{0}-1,t^{-}_{0}]
. Choosing
\lambda=t-t_{0}^{-}+1\in[0,1]
and applying (
5.91
) in Lemma
5.9
and (
5.108
), we have that

\mathcal{E}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))\leq a+\mathsf{C}^{-}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}.

(5.115)

The previous fact combined with (
5.109
), (
5.108
) and
5.114
, gives

\int_{t_{0}^{-}-1}^{t_{0}^{-}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt\leq\left(\frac{(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}}{2}+\mathsf{C}^{-}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}\right)e^{ct^{-}_{0}}+\frac{a(e^{ct^{-}_{0}}-e^{c(t_{0}^{-}-1)})}{c}.

(5.116)

The continuity of
U
and (
5.100
) implies that there exists
\tilde{t}^{-}_{2}\in(\tilde{t}^{-},t^{-}_{0})
such that

\mathrm{dist}(U(\tilde{t}_{2}^{-}),\mathscr{F}^{-})=\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{4}\mbox{ and }\forall t\in[\tilde{t}^{-},\tilde{t}^{-}_{2}],\hskip 5.69054pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})\geq\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{4}.

(5.117)

Using (
5.117
), we get

\int_{\tilde{t}^{-}}^{\tilde{t}_{2}^{-}}\lVert U^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}e^{ct}dt\geq\frac{r_{0}^{-}e^{c\tilde{t}^{-}}}{4}

(5.118)

and (
5.117
) also implies

\forall t\in[\tilde{t}^{-},\tilde{t}_{2}^{-}],\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(U(t))\geq\kappa^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/4}.

(5.119)

Inequalities (
5.118
) and (
5.119
) along with the definition of
\eta_{0}^{-}
in (
5.83
) and Young’s inequality give

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{t}^{-}}^{\tilde{t}_{2}^{-}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r_{0}^{-}e^{c\tilde{t}^{-}_{0}}}{4}\sqrt{2(\kappa^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/4}-a)}+a\frac{e^{c\tilde{t}_{2}^{-}}-e^{c\tilde{t}^{-}}}{c}

(5.120)

\displaystyle=e(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}e^{c\tilde{t}^{-}}+a\frac{e^{c\tilde{t}_{2}^{-}}-e^{c\tilde{t}^{-}}}{c},

(5.121)

which gets to, using also that
\tilde{t}^{-}\geq t^{-}_{0}-1
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t^{-}_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt

\displaystyle=\int_{(-\infty,t^{-}_{0}]\setminus[\tilde{t}^{-},\tilde{t}_{2}^{-}]}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt+\int_{\tilde{t}^{-}}^{\tilde{t}_{2}^{-}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt

(5.122)

\displaystyle\geq e(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}e^{c\tilde{t}^{-}}+a\frac{e^{ct^{-}_{0}}}{c}\geq(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}e^{ct_{0}^{-}}+a\frac{e^{ct^{-}_{0}}}{c}.

(5.123)

Using now (
5.116
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t^{-}_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{t^{-}_{0}-1}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt+\int_{t^{-}_{0}-1}^{t^{-}_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt

(5.125)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}}{2}+\mathsf{C}^{-}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}\right)e^{ct^{-}_{0}}+\frac{ae^{ct^{-}_{0}}}{c}.

(5.126)

Therefore, subtracting (
5.125
) from (
5.122
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t^{-}_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt-\int_{-\infty}^{t^{-}_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt

(5.127)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}}{2}+\mathsf{C}^{-}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}\right)e^{ct^{-}_{0}},

(5.128)

which is positive because (
5.85
) implies

\mathsf{C}^{-}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}\leq\frac{(\eta_{0}^{-})^{2}}{4}.

(5.130)

Since
\tilde{U}^{-}
and
U
coincide in
[t^{-}_{0},+\infty)
, the proof of the first case is concluded.

•

t_{0}^{-}>\tilde{t}+1
. In such a case, set

\tilde{U}^{-}(t):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}^{-}&\mbox{ if }t\leq\tilde{t}^{-},\\
(t-\tilde{t}^{-})U(t_{0}^{-})+(\tilde{t}^{-}+1-t)\mathbf{v}^{-}&\mbox{ if }\tilde{t}^{-}\leq t\leq\tilde{t}^{-}+1,\\
U(t^{-}_{0})&\mbox{ if }\tilde{t}^{-}+1\leq t\leq t^{-}_{0},\\
U(t)&\mbox{ if }t^{-}_{0}\leq t,\end{cases}

(5.131)

which clearly belongs to
X_{T}
and for all
t\leq t^{-}
,
U(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2}
by (
5.112
). We have that
\tilde{U}^{-}
is constant in
[\tilde{t}^{-}+1,t_{0}^{-}]
, therefore

\int_{\tilde{t}^{-}+1}^{t^{-}_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt\leq(a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-})\frac{e^{ct_{0}^{-}}-e^{c\tilde{t}^{-}+1}}{c}

(5.132)

and, due to the definitions of
\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}
in (
5.85
) and
t_{0}^{-}
in (
5.111
)

\int_{\tilde{t}^{-}+1}^{t_{0}^{-}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt\geq\min\{a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-},\kappa_{\eta_{0}^{-}}\}\frac{e^{ct_{0}^{-}}-e^{c\tilde{t}^{-}+1}}{c}\geq\int_{\tilde{t}^{-}+1}^{t^{-}_{0}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt,

(5.133)

because
\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}+a>0
by
(H7’)
and
t_{0}\geq\tilde{t}^{-}+1
by assumption. Hence

\int_{\tilde{t}^{-}+1}^{+\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}(t))dt\leq\int_{\tilde{t}^{-}+1}^{\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt.

(5.134)

Arguing as in the first case scenario, we can prove that

\int_{-\infty}^{\tilde{t}^{-}+1}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}(t))dt<\int_{-\infty}^{\tilde{t}^{-}+1}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt,

(5.135)

which concludes the proof of the second case.

To sum up, we have shown that if (
5.100
) is satisfied, then there exists
\tilde{U}^{-}
such that (
5.101
) and (
5.102
) hold, as we wanted.

Assume now that there exists
\tilde{t}^{+}>t^{+}
such that (
5.103
) holds. As before, Lemma
5.10
and the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}
, (
5.82
), imply that
t^{+}:=t^{+}(U,\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+})
is such that

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t^{+}),\mathscr{F}^{+})\leq\eta_{0}^{+}

(5.136)

and

\mathcal{E}(U(t^{+}))\leq\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}.

(5.137)

We claim that we can assume without loss of generality that

\forall t\in[t^{+},+\infty),\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(U(t))\geq 0.

(5.138)

Indeed, if we can find
t_{0}\in(t^{+},+\infty)
such that
\mathcal{E}(U(t))<0
, then by
(H7’)
we have have that
\mathcal{E}(U(t_{0}))\leq a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}
and by (
4.38
) in
(H7’)
we also have that
\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t_{0}),\mathscr{F}^{-})\leq r_{0}^{-}/2
. Therefore, we have by the definitions (
5.86
) and (
5.87
) that
t^{-}\geq t_{0}
and
t^{+}>t^{-}
, a contradiction since we assume
t_{0}>t^{+}
.

For the positive case, the proof is simpler as it suffices to define
\mathbf{v}^{+}:=P^{+}(U(t^{+}))
and

\tilde{U}^{+}(t):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}^{+}&\mbox{ if }t\geq t^{+}+1\\
(t-t^{+})\mathbf{v}^{+}+(t^{+}+1-t)U(t^{+})&\mbox{ if }t^{+}+1\geq t\geq t^{+},\\
U(t)&\mbox{ if }t^{+}\geq t,\end{cases}

(5.139)

which is such that
U\in X_{T}
. Moreover, it holds that for all
t\geq t^{+}
, we have
\tilde{U}^{+}(t)\in\mathscr{F}^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2}
. Therefore, the requirements (
5.104
) and (
5.105
) hold for
\tilde{U}^{+}
. It remains to check that (
5.105
) is also fulfilled. We have that

\int_{t^{+}}^{t^{+}+1}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{+}(t))dt=\int_{t^{+}}^{t^{+}+1}\left[\frac{\left\lVert U(t^{+})-\mathbf{v}^{+}\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{2}+\mathcal{E}(\tilde{U}^{+}(t))\right]e^{ct}dt.

(5.140)

Using (
5.91
) in Lemma
5.9
and (
5.137
), we get

\int_{t^{+}}^{t^{+}+1}\mathcal{E}(\tilde{U}^{+}(t))e^{ct}dt\leq\mathsf{C}^{+}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}e^{c(t^{+}+1)}.

(5.141)

Using now (
5.136
), we get

\int_{t^{+}}^{t^{+}+1}\frac{\left\lVert U(t^{+})-\mathbf{v}^{+}\right\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}}{2}e^{ct}dt\leq\frac{(\eta_{0}^{+})^{2}}{2}e^{c(t^{+}+1)}.

(5.142)

Plugging (
5.141
) and (
5.142
) into (
5.140
), we get

\int_{t^{+}}^{t^{+}+1}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{+}(t))dt\leq\left(\frac{(\eta_{0}^{+})^{2}}{2}+\mathsf{C}^{+}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}\right)e^{ct^{+}+1}.

(5.143)

Since for all
t\geq t^{+}+1
we have
\tilde{U}^{+}(t)=\mathbf{v}^{+}
, we obtain from (
5.143
)

\int_{t^{+}}^{+\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{+}(t))dt\leq\left(\frac{(\eta_{0}^{+})^{2}}{2}+\mathsf{C}^{+}\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}\right)e^{ct^{+}+1}.

(5.144)

Next, notice that by continuity we can find
\tilde{t}_{2}^{+}\in(t^{+},\tilde{t}^{+})
such that

\mathrm{dist}(U(\tilde{t}_{2}^{+}),\mathscr{F}^{+})=\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{4}\mbox{ and }\forall t\in[\tilde{t}^{+},\tilde{t}_{2}^{+}],\hskip 5.69054pt\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2}\geq\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{+})\geq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{4}.

(5.145)

Therefore, using (
5.103
) and (
5.145
), we obtain

\int_{\tilde{t_{2}}^{+}}^{\tilde{t}^{+}}\lVert U^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}e^{ct}dt\geq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{4}e^{ct^{+}+1}e^{-1}

(5.146)

and (
5.145
), (
4.7
) in
(H1’)
imply

\forall t\in[\tilde{t}^{+},\tilde{t}_{2}^{+}],\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(U(t))\geq\kappa_{r_{0}^{+}/4}^{+}.

(5.147)

Inequalities (
5.146
), (
5.147
) yield by Young’s inequality

\int_{\tilde{t}_{2}^{+}}^{\tilde{t}^{+}}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt\geq\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{4}e^{ct^{+}+1}e^{-1}\sqrt{2\kappa_{r_{0}^{+}/4}^{+}}=(\eta_{0}^{+})^{2}e^{t^{+}+1},

(5.148)

where the last equality is due to the definition of
\eta_{0}^{+}
in (
5.80
). Combining with (
5.138
), we get

\int_{t^{+}}^{+\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt\geq(\eta_{0}^{+})^{2}e^{t^{+}+1}.

(5.149)

The definition of
\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+}
in (
5.82
) and (
5.144
) imply then that

\int_{t^{+}}^{+\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(U(t))dt>\int_{t^{+}}^{+\infty}\mathbf{e}_{c}(\tilde{U}^{-}(t))dt,

(5.150)

which establishes (
5.105
).

We now show the last part of the proof: we show that (
5.106
) holds with the constant
T_{\star}(c)
defined in (
5.107
). The argument is the same as in
[
6
]
,
Lemma 2.10
. Assume by contradiction that there exists
t\in(t^{-},+\infty)
such that
\mathcal{E}(U(t))<0
. Then, arguing as above we must have
t<t^{-}
by the definition of
t^{-}
in (
5.86
), a contradiction. Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lVert U^{\prime}(t)\rVert_{\mathscr{L}}^{2}e^{ct}dt-\int_{-\infty}^{t^{-}}\mathcal{E}^{-}(U(t))e^{ct}dt

(5.151)

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{E}^{+}(U(t))e^{ct}dt,

(5.152)

where
\mathcal{E}^{-}
and
\mathcal{E}^{+}
stand for the positive and the negative part of
\mathcal{E}
, respectively. We have that

\int_{-\infty}^{t^{-}}\mathcal{E}^{-}(U(t))e^{ct}dt\leq\frac{-a}{c}e^{ct^{-}}.

(5.153)

Set
\alpha_{\star}:=\min\{\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{+},\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}+a\}>0
, which is independent on
U
,
c
and
T
. Notice that for all
t\in(t^{-},t^{+})
we have that
\mathcal{E}(U(t))\geq\alpha_{\star}
. Indeed, if
\mathcal{E}(U(t))<\alpha_{\star}
, then by the definition of
t^{-}
and
t^{+}
in (
5.86
) and (
5.87
) we get

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(U(t),\mathscr{F}^{\pm})\geq\frac{r_{0}^{\pm}}{2},

(5.154)

which implies that

\mathcal{E}(U(t))\geq\min\{\kappa^{+}_{r_{0}^{+}/2},\kappa^{-}_{r_{0}^{-}/2}+a\}\geq\alpha_{\star},

(5.155)

by (
5.85
) and (
5.82
), a contradiction. Therefore,

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{E}^{+}(U(t))e^{ct}dt\geq\int_{t^{-}}^{t^{+}}\mathcal{E}^{+}(U(t))e^{ct}dt\geq\frac{\alpha_{\star}}{c}\left(e^{ct^{+}}-e^{ct^{-}}\right).

(5.156)

Plugging (
5.153
) and (
5.156
) into (
5.151
) and using that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)\leq 0
, we obtain

0\geq\frac{a}{c}e^{ct^{-}}+\frac{\alpha_{\star}}{c}\left(e^{ct^{+}}-e^{ct^{-}}\right)\geq\left(\frac{a}{c}+\frac{\alpha_{\star}}{c}\left(e^{c(t^{+}-t^{-})}-1\right)\right)e^{ct^{-}},

(5.157)

that is,

0\geq-\left(\frac{-a}{\alpha_{\star}}+1\right)+e^{c(t^{+}-t^{-})},

(5.158)

which implies

0<t^{+}-t^{-}\leq\frac{1}{c}\ln\left(\frac{-a}{\alpha_{\star}}+1\right)=\mathsf{T}_{\star}(c),

(5.159)

which is exactly (
5.106
) according to the definition (
5.107
).
∎

Figure 5.2:
As it has been shown, the proof of Proposition
5.1
consists on showing that if the function
U
gets too far from
\mathscr{F}^{\pm}
after getting too close, then we can find a suitable competitor with strictly less energy. Above, we see a design for the positive case (the competitor
\tilde{U}^{+}
is represented in blue). The second and third picture correspond to the two possible scenarios for the negative case (the competitor
\tilde{U}^{-}
is represented in blue).

The importance of Proposition
5.1
is summarized by the following result, which gives important information on the behavior of the constrained minimizers:

Corollary 5.1
.

Assume that
(H3’)
,
(H4’)
,
(H5’)
and
(H7’)
hold. Let
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. Let
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}
be an associated minimizer of
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in
X_{T}
given by Lemma
5.7
. Then, if
t^{\pm}:=t^{\pm}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T},\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{\pm})
are as in (
5.86
), (
5.87
) it holds that

\forall t\leq t^{-},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})<\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}

(5.160)

and

\forall t\geq t^{+},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t),\mathscr{F}^{+})<\frac{r_{0}^{+}}{2},

(5.161)

Moreover, we have

\forall t\geq t^{-},\hskip 5.69054pt\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t))\geq 0.

(5.162)

Finally, we have that if
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})\leq 0
, then

0<t^{+}-t^{-}\leq\mathsf{T}_{\star}(c),

(5.163)

where
\mathsf{T}_{\star}(c)
is as in (
5.107
). In particular, the function

c\in(0,+\infty)\to\mathsf{T}_{\star}(c)

(5.164)

is continuous.

Proof.

If we assume by contradiction that (
5.161
) does not hold, then we necessarily have that there exists
\tilde{t}^{-}<t^{-}
such that (
5.100
) holds. Proposition
5.1
implies then the existence of
\tilde{U}\in X_{T}
such that
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\tilde{U})<\mathbf{E}_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})=\mathbf{m}_{c,T}
, a contradiction. Therefore, (
5.161
) holds. Similarly, we can show that (
5.160
) also holds. Finally, in order to establish (
5.162
), we argue as in the proof Proposition
5.1
. Indeed, due to the definition of
t^{-}
, we have that for
t\geq t^{-}
it holds that either

\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t))\geq a+\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{-}>0

(5.165)

(which is
(H7’)
) or

\mathrm{dist}_{\mathscr{L}}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t),\mathscr{F}^{-})\geq\frac{r_{0}^{-}}{2}

(5.166)

which by (
4.38
) in
(H7’)
implies that
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T}(t))\geq 0
. Therefore, (
5.162
) holds and the proof is concluded.
∎

Moreover, Lemma
5.8
applies to
\mathbf{V}_{c,T}
as follows:

Corollary 5.2
.

Assume that
(H3’)
,
(H4’)
,
(H5’)
and
(H7’)
hold. Let
c>0
and
T\geq 1
. Let
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}
be an associated minimizer of
\mathbf{E}_{c}
in
X_{T}
given by Lemma
5.7
. Then, if
t^{\pm}:=t^{\pm}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T},\mathcal{E}_{\max}^{\pm})
are as in (
5.86
), (
5.87
) it holds that there exists
\delta_{c,T}>0
such that the set

S_{c,T}:=(-\infty,t^{-}+\delta_{c,T})\cup(-T,T)\cup(t^{+}-\delta_{c,T},+\infty)

(5.167)

is such that
\mathbf{U}_{c,T}\in\mathcal{A}(S_{c,T})
(see (
4.29
)) and

\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime\prime}-D_{\mathscr{L}}\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{U}_{c,T})=-c\mathbf{U}_{c,T}^{\prime}\mbox{ in }S_{c,T}.

(5.168)

The proof of
5.2
is obtained in a straightforward manner by combining Lemma
5.8
with the informations given by Corollary
5.1
. Notice that
5.2
implies that constrained solutions are picewise solutions and, in particular, they solve the equation for times with large absolute value.

5.7
Existence of the unconstrained solutions

We now establish the existence of the unconstrained solutions making use of the previous comparison results. As in
[
6
]
and
[
7
]
, we define the set

\mathcal{C}:=\{c>0:\exists T\geq 1\mbox{ and }U\in X_{T}\mbox{ such that }\mathbf{E}_{c}(U)<0\}.

(5.169)

We first prove some important properties for
\mathcal{C}
which are the same to
Lemma 2.12
in
[
6
]
and
Lemma 27
in
[
7
]
:

Lemma 5.11
.

Assume that
(H3’)
,
(H4’)
and
(H5’)
hold. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the set defined in (
5.169
). Then,
\mathcal{C}
is open and non-empty. Moreover, if we assume that
(H7’)
holds, then
\mathcal{C}
is also bounded with

\sup\mathcal{C}\leq\frac{\sqrt{-2a}}{d_{0}},

(5.170)

where
d_{0}
was defined in (
4.36
).

Proof.

Firstly, we show that
\mathcal{C}\not=\emptyset
. For that purpose, consider the function
\Psi
introduced in (
5.16
). Consider the function

f:c\in(0,+\infty)\to e^{-c}\left(\frac{a}{c}+e^{2c}\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\frac{\lVert\Psi^{\prime}(t)\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}}}{2}+\mathcal{E}(\Psi(t))\right)dt\right)\in{\mathbb{R}},

(5.171)

which is well defined by Lemma
5.4
. We have that for all
c>0

\mathbf{E}_{c}(\Psi)=\frac{-a}{c}e^{-c}+\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\frac{\lVert\Psi^{\prime}(t)\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}}}{2}+\mathcal{E}(\Psi(t))\right)e^{ct}dt\leq f(c)

(5.172)

and
f
is a continuous function such that
\lim_{c\to 0}f(c)=-\infty
because
a<0
. Moreover, we have that for all
c>0
,

f^{\prime}(c)=-e^{-c}a+ce^{2c}\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\frac{\lVert\Psi^{\prime}(t)\rVert^{2}_{\mathscr{L}}}{2}+\mathcal{E}(\Psi(t))\right)dt>0

(5.173)

and
\lim_{c\to+\infty}f(c)=+\infty
. Therefore, there exists a unique
c_{\Psi}>0
such that
f(c_{\Psi})=0
and for all
c<c_{\Psi}
we have
\mathbf{E}_{c}(\Psi)<0
by (
5.172
). Therefore,
(0,c_{\Psi})\subset\mathcal{C}
, meaning that
\mathcal{C}\not=\emptyset
as we wanted to show.

We next prove that
\mathcal{C}
is open. Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
, we have that
